is the amplitude of the curvature power spectrum. In terms of the dark-matter density contrast, Eq. (
130
) implies that
\Delta_{\delta}^{2}<8.0\times 10^{-11}
at CMB scales.

For dark matter arising from CGPP, the scale and shape of the isocurvature power spectrum depends on the spin, mass, and gravitational coupling of particles. For instance, a conformally coupled scalar field has
\Delta_{\delta}^{2}\propto k^{3}
, which may give
O(1)
isocurvature at small scales, but it implies a negligible isocurvature at CMB scales; see Sec.
IV.4
. A similar result is obtained for fields with nonzero spin, which have blue-tilted spectra; see Sec.
V
. Therefore, CMB limits on isocurvature are most constraining for models of minimally coupled (
\xi=0
) scalar dark matter. If the dark particles are light (
m\ll H_{e}\lesssim H_{\mathrm{inf}}
), then the power spectrum is nearly scale invariant (see Sec.
IV.4
), and its amplitude at CMB scales comes into conflict with the CMB limits, but lifting the dark matter’s mass to be
m\gtrsim H_{e}
tilts the power spectrum toward blue and evades the isocurvature constraints
Chung et al. 2005
. Alternatively, introducing a nonminimal coupling to gravity also leads to a blue tilt and evades these constraints
Kolb et al. 2023b
;
Garcia et al. 2023b
, even for
\xi=O(0.01)
. These models have inspired searches for blue-tilted isocurvature in cosmological observables
Chung 2016
;
Chung and Upadhye 2017
;
Chung and Upadhye 2018
.

VI.14
Observational: non-Gaussianity

Observations of the CMB are compatible with a spectrum of primordial curvature perturbations such that the amplitude of each Fourier mode can be modeled as an independent stochastic (random) variable with a Gaussian probability distribution centered at zero. These Gaussian initial conditions are a hallmark prediction of single-field slow-roll inflation
Mukhanov et al. 1992
. Their Gaussian nature follows from the fact that the inflaton is weakly coupled to itself and other fields, and a free scalar field has a Gaussian wave functional in its ground state
Maldacena 2003
. If the theory is extended to include nongravitational interactions, the primordial curvature perturbations develop a larger non-Gaussian component. This non-Gaussianity manifests in CMB anisotropy observables at the level of three-point (and higher-point) correlation functions, which are known as the bispectrum (trispectrum, etc.). Measurements of the CMB anisotropies are compatible with Gaussian initial conditions and thereby constrain theories that predict appreciable non-Gaussianity
Akrami et al. 2020a
.

In theories that extend the inflaton sector to include additional particles at the same mass scale, which is known as quasi-single-field inflation, an appreciable non-Gaussianity can develop in the curvature perturbations
Chen and Wang 2010b
;
Chen and Wang 2010a
;
Chen 2010
;
Baumann and Green 2012
;
Noumi et al. 2013
;
Arkani-Hamed and Maldacena 2015
;
Baumann et al. 2018
;
Arkani-Hamed et al. 2020
. In this way inflationary observables act as a “cosmological collider” experiment to probe particles and forces whose high mass scale puts them beyond the reach of terrestrial high-energy experiments.

In theories where dark matter arises from CGPP, it is natural to consider new particle species having masses around the inflationary Hubble scale
m_{\chi}\approx H_{\mathrm{inf}}
. For instance, the gravitational production of spin-
\nicefrac{{1\mkern-2.2mu}}{{2}}
particles is suppressed for
m_{\chi}\ll H_{\mathrm{inf}}
since the fermion mass scale is the source of conformal symmetry breaking; see Sec.
V.2
.
Chung and Yoo 2013
[see also
Hikage et al. 2009
;
Chung et al. 2015
;
Li et al. 2019
] found that the same theories in which dark matter is produced via CGPP can also lead to an appreciable CMB non-Gaussianity in the limit of the squeezed local triangle form. For instance, a benchmark parameter point predicts that
f_{\text{\sc nl}}^{\mathrm{local}}\sim 30
, which is now excluded by Planck’s constraint
f_{\text{\sc nl}}^{\mathrm{local}}=-0.9\pm 5.1

Akrami et al. 2020a
. CGPP can also lead to rare “hot spots” in the CMB
Kim et al. 2021
;
Kim et al. 2023
. These examples illustrate the capability of current and future probes of CMB non-Gaussianity to test CGPP.

VI.15
Observational: small-scale structure

If the particles that experience CGPP are going to provide a viable candidate for dark matter, then they must be sufficiently cold at the time of radiation-matter equality. For superheavy particles with
m_{X}\approx H_{e}
this is easily accomplished. Even if the spectrum after production is peaked at a high comoving wave number, say
k\approx a_{e}H_{e}
corresponding to Hubble-scale modes at the end of inflation, the characteristic velocity today is still extremely small:

\displaystyle v_{X}

\displaystyle=k/a_{0}m_{X}\approx 5\times 10^{-32}\biggl(\frac{H_{e}}{10^{14}\,\mathrm{GeV}}\biggr)^{1/3}

(131)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\biggl(\frac{T_{\text{\sc rh}}}{10^{9}\,\mathrm{GeV}}\biggr)^{1/3}\biggl(\frac{m_{X}}{10^{14}\,\mathrm{GeV}}\biggr)^{-1}\ .\

In Eq. (
131
) the redshift factors are calculated by assuming a matter-dominated epoch of reheating (
w_{\text{\sc rh}}=0
). However, for models in which gravitational production remains efficient even for
m_{X}\ll H_{e}
, the velocity can be much larger. For instance, taking
m_{X}=10^{-12}\,\mathrm{GeV}=\mathrm{meV}
gives
v_{X}\approx 0.5\times 10^{-5}\approx 1.5\ \mathrm{km}/\mathrm{sec}
.

Although
v_{X}\approx 10^{-5}
still corresponds to extremely nonrelativistic particles, there is nevertheless a problem with such “fast” dark matter. For successful structure formation, dark-matter particles must cluster through their mutual gravitational attraction, thereby allowing overdensities to grow and form halos. However, if dark matter travels too quickly it will “escape” from its initial overdensity before structures can form. In an FLRW spacetime a free particle travels a distance

\displaystyle l_{\mathrm{fs}}(t)=a(t)\int_{0}^{t}\!\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,\frac{v(t^{\prime})}{a(t^{\prime})}=a\int_{0}^{a}\!\mathrm{d}a^{\prime}\,\frac{v(a^{\prime})}{(a^{\prime})^{2}H(a^{\prime})}

(132)

in time
t
called the free-streaming length. For the values of
H_{e}
,
T_{\text{\sc rh}}
, and
m_{X}=\mathrm{meV}
used in Eq. (
132
), the free-streaming length today is
l_{\mathrm{fs}}(t_{0})\approx 20\,\mathrm{Mpc}
, and it accumulates mostly during the radiation era. The free-streaming length indicates the size of structures whose formation are inhibited by the dark matter’s speediness.

There are various probes of cosmologically small-scale structure in the spatial distribution of matter
Tulin and Yu 2018
;
Drlica-Wagner et al. 2022
. For instance, observations of Lyman-
\alpha
absorption due to intergalactic neutral hydrogen in the spectra of distant quasars,
i.e.
, the Lyman-
\alpha
forest, are compatible with an
\Lambda
CDM-like matter power spectrum at wave numbers below
k\approx 10a_{0}h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}

Viel et al. 2013
. These measurements place upper bounds on the free-streaming length of dark matter, which are frequently used to constrain warm dark matter
Iršič et al. 2017
, but they also lead to constraints on
m_{X}
in the CGPP production scenario. A recent study by
Garcia et al. 2023b
assessed the impact of Lyman-
\alpha
observations on a theory of nonminimally coupled scalar dark matter that is formed by CGPP during inflation and reheating; see also
Haque and Maity 2022
;
Garcia et al. 2023d
. Their parameter space constraints are reproduced here as Fig.
10
. Observe that gravitationally produced dark matter with a mass below
m_{\chi}\sim 10^{-12}\,\mathrm{GeV}
(rising to
10^{-7}\,\mathrm{GeV}
for larger nonminimal coupling
\xi
) would be in conflict with Lyman-
\alpha
observations and is thereby ruled out.

VI.16
Observational: signatures of late time decay

If the particles arising from CGPP are unstable but long-lived, then their late-time decay into visible-sector radiation could lead to signatures in cosmological and astrophysical observables. Here we remark on three possible probes of such an energy injection: nucleosynthesis, CMB spectrum and anisotropies, and cosmic rays.

The observed abundances of the light elements are compatible with the predictions of BBN
Cyburt et al. 2016
. The standard BBN calculation assumes that the universe is filled with a plasma of standard-model particles at temperatures around a few mega-electron-volts. This good agreement between observation and theory leads to constraints on the presence of an additional particle species that decay and inject energy into the plasma
Cyburt et al. 2003
;
Kawasaki et al. 2018
;
Fong et al. 2022
.

The energy spectrum of the CMB is observed to agree well with a Planck thermal blackbody distribution
Chluba et al. 2019
. In the standard
\Lambda
CDM cosmology, this thermal distribution arises before recombination because photons couple to the electron-ion plasma through single-Compton and double-Compton scattering, which leads to a common temperature for all species and a vanishing chemical potential for the photon. However, double-Compton scattering goes out of equilibrium before single-Compton scattering, and during this time interval the photon number is approximately conserved. An energy injection would then lead to a nonzero photon chemical potential (spectral distortion)
Hu and Silk 1993
, which is constrained by measurements of the CMB energy spectrum by COBE/FIRAS
Fixsen et al. 1996
. Similarly, energy injection from large-scale inhomogeneities can leave an imprint on CMB anisotropies
Ali-Haïmoud and Kamionkowski 2017
;
Acharya and Khatri 2019
.

If superheavy particles are decaying in the present-day Universe, their visible-sector decay products would correspond to highly energetic photons, protons, electrons, positrons, and neutrinos. Of particular interest is the production of ultra-high-energy cosmic ray (UHECR) protons above the Greisen-Zatsepin-Kuzmin (GZK) cutoff
E_{\mathrm{cr}}\sim 10^{10}\,\mathrm{GeV}

Greisen 1966
;
Zatsepin and Kuzmin 1966
. Since protons scatter on CMB photons via a
\Delta
resonance, the Universe is not transparent to UHECR protons on cosmological distances. Conversely, a detection of UHECR protons could be interpreted as evidence for a nearby source, such as superheavy dark-matter decays within the local halo
Kuzmin and Rubakov 1998
;
Kuzmin and Tkachev 1998
;
Kuzmin and Tkachev 1999
. More generally, searches with cosmic ray, gamma ray, and neutrino telescopes are probing long-lived superheavy dark matter
Aloisio et al. 2015
;
Kachelriess et al. 2018
;
Ishiwata et al. 2020
;
Chianese et al. 2021a
;
Chianese et al. 2021b
;
Das et al. 2023
.

VII
Outlook

How does a strong gravitational field lead to particle creation? What are the cosmological implications of these particles and their observational signatures? For nearly the past 100 years, these questions have guided the study of cosmological gravitational particle production. Today, a vibrant community of researchers is working to enrich our knowledge of CGPP as a mechanism and to explore novel applications and implications of this phenomenon. With this review we hope to provide a tool kit that another generation of researchers can employ to pursue their own studies of CGPP, derive predictions for cosmological relics, explore the implications for new directions beyond the standard model, and propose observational tests.

Acknowledgements.

We thank our colleagues and collaborators for feedback over the many years while this review was taking shape. We appreciate Mustafa Amin, Daniel Chung, Leah Jenks, Siyang Ling, Evan McDonough, and Sarunas Verner for taking the time to provide comments on a draft of the article. E.W.K. acknowledges the hospitality of The Institute for Theoretical Physics in Madrid, where part of this work was done. The work of E.W.K. was supported in part by U.S. Department of Energy Contract No. DE-FG02-13ER41958. A.J.L. is supported in part by the National Science Foundation under Award No. PHY-2114024.

Appendix A
General relativity

This appendix provides a refresher on general relativity (GR), the relativistic field theory of gravity that describes its interactions with radiation and matter. There are many notable textbooks on GR, including:

Gravitation and Cosmology

Weinberg 1972
,

Gravitation

Misner et al. 1973
,

The Classical Theory of Fields

Landau and Lifschits 1980
,

General Relativity

Wald 1984
,

Quantum field theory in curved spacetime and black hole thermodynamics

Wald 1995

Geometry, Topology, and Physics

Nakahara 2003
,
Supergravity

Freedman and Van Proeyen 2012
, and

Spacetime and Geometry

Carroll 2019
.

.

Sign and unit conventions.

Different researchers adopt different conventions when writing the equations of GR. A table summarizing frequently encountered sign choices was provided by
Misner et al. 1973
, and an appendix of
Freedman and Van Proeyen 2012
included additional signs and phases that arise in the frame-field formalism. In these appendixes we follow
Freedman and Van Proeyen 2012
and use the symbols
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\mathrm{s}_{\ast}
,
\mathrm{s}_{1}
,
\mathrm{s}_{2}
,
\cdots
,
\mathrm{s}_{8}
to simultaneously track the various sign choices; one can set
\alpha=\pm 1,\pm\mathrm{i}
,
\beta=\pm 1
, and
\mathrm{s}_{i}=\pm 1
. In the body of the review we take
-\mathrm{i}\alpha=\beta=-\mathrm{s}_{\ast}=-\mathrm{s}_{1}=\mathrm{s}_{2}=\mathrm{s}_{3}=\mathrm{s}_{4}=\mathrm{s}_{6}=\mathrm{s}_{8}=1
; we do not use
\mathrm{s}_{5}
or
\mathrm{s}_{7}
. This corresponds to
(-,+,+)
in the notation of
Misner et al. 1973
. In addition, we work in natural units, setting the speed of light
c
and the reduced Planck constant
\hbar
equal to
1
.

.

Spacetime and metric.

The coordinates of four-dimensional spacetime are denoted by
x^{\mu}
for
\mu=0,1,2,3
. Gravity is described by a type
(0,2)
tensor field
{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}(x)
, called the metric, that may vary throughout spacetime. The inverse metric field is denoted by
{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}(x)
and defined by
{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}(x){\textit{g}}_{\nu\rho}(x)=\delta_{\rho}^{\mu}
, where
\delta_{\rho}^{\mu}
is the Kronecker delta symbol. The determinant of the metric is denoted by
g
and appears in the factor
\sqrt{-g}
. The metric gives the differential invariant line element
(\mathrm{d}s)^{2}={\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}(x)\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}
. In Minkowski spacetime the metric is static, homogeneous, and isotropic; it can be written as

\displaystyle{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}^{\mathrm{Mink}}(x)=\eta_{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{s}_{1}\,\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,1,1)\ ,\

(133)

and one can take
\mathrm{s}_{1}=\pm 1
. Its inverse is
{\textit{g}}_{\mathrm{Mink}}^{\mu\nu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}
.

.

Curvature definitions.

Objects that arise in the definition of spacetime curvature include the Levi-Civita connection (torsion-free affine connection)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\rho}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\textit{g}}^{\rho\lambda}\bigl(\partial_{\mu}{\textit{g}}_{\nu\lambda}+\partial_{\nu}{\textit{g}}_{\mu\lambda}-\partial_{\lambda}{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}\bigr)\ ,\

(134)

the Riemann curvature tensor

\begin{split}\mathchoice{R^{{{\rho}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.50554pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.50554pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.17293pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.83778pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma\mu\nu}}}}{R^{{{\rho}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.50554pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.50554pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.17293pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.83778pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma\mu\nu}}}}{R^{{{\rho}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.50554pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.50554pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.17293pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.83778pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma\mu\nu}}}}{R^{{{\rho}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.50554pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.50554pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.17293pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.83778pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma\mu\nu}}}}(x)&=\mathrm{s}_{2}\,\Bigl(\partial_{\mu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\nu\sigma}-\partial_{\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\sigma}\\
&\qquad\quad+\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\lambda}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\nu\sigma}-\Gamma^{\rho}_{\nu\lambda}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\sigma}\Bigr)\ ,\ \end{split}

(135)

the Ricci tensor
R_{\mu\nu}(x)=(\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{3})^{-1}\mathchoice{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.50754pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0768pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\alpha\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.50754pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0768pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\alpha\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.50754pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0768pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\alpha\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.50754pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0768pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\alpha\nu}}}}
, the Ricci scalar
R(x)={\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}
, and the Einstein tensor
G_{\mu\nu}(x)=R_{\mu\nu}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}R
. The totally-antisymmetric Levi-Civita connection
\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
is normalized as
\varepsilon_{0123}=\mathrm{s}_{5}=\pm 1
.

.

Covariant derivatives.

Derivatives that transform covariantly under coordinate transformations (diffeomorphisms) are denoted by
\nabla_{\!\!\mu}
. Covariant derivatives of scalar, vector, and rank-2 tensor fields are defined by

\displaystyle\nabla_{\!\!\sigma}S=\partial_{\sigma}S

(136a)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\!\!\sigma}V^{\mu}=\partial_{\sigma}V^{\mu}+\Gamma^{\mu}_{\sigma\rho}V^{\rho}

(136b)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\!\!\sigma}V_{\mu}=\partial_{\sigma}V_{\mu}-\Gamma^{\rho}_{\sigma\mu}V_{\rho}

(136c)

kebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\rho}{\nu}}}}{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\rho}{\nu}}}}-\Gamma^{\rho}_{\sigma\nu}\mathchoice{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\rho}}}}{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\rho}}}}{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\rho}}}}{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\rho}}}}\ ,\

(136g)

and the generalization to higher-rank tensors is obvious. When applied to a type
(p,q)
tensor with
p
contravariant (up) indices and
q
covariant (down) indices, the covariant derivative produces a type
(p,q+1)
tensor. The Levi-Civita connection parallel transports the metric
\nabla_{\!\!\sigma}{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}=0
. The d’Alembertian operator of a scalar field
\Phi
is

\begin{split}\Box\Phi={\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\!\!\mu}\nabla_{\!\!\nu}\Phi=(\sqrt{-g})^{-1}\partial_{\mu}(\sqrt{-g}\,{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\Phi)\ .\ \end{split}

(137)

.

Einstein-Hilbert action and matter.

The dynamics of gravity and its interactions with radiation and matter are encoded in an action
S
using the formalism of Lagrangian mechanics. We treat the radiation and matter as classical fields, and denote them collectively by
F_{i}(x)
. We study a class of theories whose actions take the form

\displaystyle S[F_{i}(x),{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}(x)]=S_{\text{\sc eh}}[{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}(x)]+S_{\text{\sc m}}[F_{i}(x),{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}(x)]\ ,\

(138)

where
S_{\text{\sc eh}}
is the Einstein-Hilbert (EH) action and
S_{\text{\sc m}}
is the radiation-matter action. The EH action is

\displaystyle S_{\text{\sc eh}}[{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}(x)]=\mathrm{s}_{1}\mathrm{s}_{3}\,\frac{1}{2}M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}\int\!\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\sqrt{-g}\,R\ ,\

(139)

where
M_{\mathrm{Pl}}
is the reduced Planck mass,
G_{N}=1/8\pi M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}
is Newton’s gravitational constant, and
\kappa^{2}=8\pi G_{N}
is the gravitational coupling. The radiation-matter action takes the form

\displaystyle S_{\text{\sc m}}[F_{i}(x),{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}(x)]=\int\!\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\sqrt{-g}\,\mathcal{L}\ ,\

(140)

where
\mathcal{L}
is the radiation-matter Lagrangian density.

.

Einstein’s equation.

The equation of motion for the metric, known as Einstein’s equation, is derived by applying the variational principle. Imposing
\delta S/\delta{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}=0
leads to Einstein’s equation
Einstein 1916

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{3}\,G_{\mu\nu}=8\pi G_{N}\,T_{\mu\nu}=M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{-2}\,T_{\mu\nu}=\kappa^{2}\,T_{\mu\nu}\ .\

(141)

When evaluating the variational derivative of the Einstein-Hilbert term, the following identities prove useful:

\displaystyle\delta{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-{\textit{g}}^{\mu\rho}{\textit{g}}^{\nu\sigma}\,\delta{\textit{g}}_{\rho\sigma}

(142a)

\displaystyle\delta{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-{\textit{g}}_{\mu\rho}{\textit{g}}_{\nu\sigma}\delta{\textit{g}}^{\rho\sigma}

(142b)

\displaystyle\delta g

\displaystyle=g\,{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}\delta{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}

(142c)

\displaystyle\delta\sqrt{-g}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{-g}\,{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}\,\delta{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}

(142d)

\displaystyle\delta R

\displaystyle=-R^{\mu\nu}\delta{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}+\mathrm{s}_{3}\bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}{\textit{g}}^{\rho\mu}{\textit{g}}^{\sigma\nu}

(142e)

\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{2}{\textit{g}}^{\rho\nu}{\textit{g}}^{\sigma\mu}-{\textit{g}}^{\rho\sigma}{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}\bigr)\nabla_{\!\!\nu}\nabla_{\!\!\mu}\delta{\textit{g}}_{\rho\sigma}\ ,\

and it follows that
\delta S_{\text{\sc eh}}/\delta{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{s}_{1}\mathrm{s}_{3}(\sqrt{-g}/2)M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}G_{\mu\nu}
.

.

Stress-energy tensor.

The stress-energy tensor field
T_{\mu\nu}(x)
is defined as the variational derivative of the radiation-matter action

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{s}_{1}\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g}}\,\frac{\delta S_{\text{\sc m}}}{\delta{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}}\ .\

(143)

Note that the stress-energy tensor is manifestly symmetric since the inverse metric is symmetric. The scalar
\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}={\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}T_{\nu\mu}
is called the trace of the stress-energy tensor. For theories with fields of half-integer spin, the matter action is expressed in terms of the frame fields
e_{a}^{\mu}(x)
(see the forthcoming discussion) rather than the metric, and the stress-energy tensor is calculated using

\displaystyle T{{}_{a}^{\mu}}=\frac{1}{e}\frac{\delta S_{\text{\sc m}}}{\delta e{{}^{a}_{\mu}}}\quad\text{and}\quad T_{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{s}_{1}{\textit{g}}_{\mu\rho}{\textit{g}}_{\nu\sigma}\eta^{ab}e_{a}^{\rho}T_{b}^{\sigma}\ .\

(144)

.

Perfect fluid stress-energy tensor.

In many applications the radiation-matter sector is modeled as a perfect fluid. In such systems the stress-energy tensor takes the form

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}=\bigl(\rho+P\bigr)u_{\mu}u_{\nu}+\mathrm{s}_{1}P\,{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}\ ,\

(145)

where
\rho(x)
is the scalar energy density field,
P(x)
is the scalar pressure field, and
u_{\mu}(x)
is the vector velocity field. The vector field is normalized such that
{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}u_{\mu}u_{\nu}=-\mathrm{s}_{1}
. It follows that
\rho=T^{\mu\nu}u_{\mu}u_{\nu}
and
3P=\mathrm{s}_{1}\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}+\rho

.

Canonical stress-energy tensor.

For this discussion we restrict our attention to Minkowski spacetime. If spacetime translations are a symmetry of the theory, the four Noether currents form a rank-2 tensor called the canonical stress-energy tensor, which we denote by
\Theta^{\mu\nu}
. These currents may be derived by applying Noether’s theorem; see
Weinberg 2005
for a review. For a theory of fields
F_{i}(x)
one finds that

\displaystyle\Theta^{\mu\nu}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial(\partial_{\mu}F_{i})}\eta^{\nu\rho}\partial_{\rho}F_{i}-\eta^{\mu\nu}\mathcal{L}\ .\

(146)

Although the canonical stress-energy tensor is conserved (
\partial_{\mu}\Theta^{\mu\nu}=0
), it need not be symmetric or even gauge invariant. However if Lorentz invariance is also a symmetry of the theory, then one can construct a conserved and symmetric tensor, the Belinfante-Rosenfeld stress-energy tensor, and denote it by
t^{\mu\nu}
. The relation takes the form
t^{\mu\nu}=\Theta^{\mu\nu}+\partial_{\rho}B^{\rho\mu\nu}
, where
B^{\rho\mu\nu}=-B^{\mu\rho\nu}
is antisymmetric in its
first
two indices
Belinfante 1939
;
Belinfante 1940
;
Rosenfeld 1940
. In Minkowski spacetime the Belinfante-Rosenfeld stress-energy tensor
t^{\mu\nu}
coincides with the Hilbert stress-energy tensor
T^{\mu\nu}
defined in Eq. (
143
) when the action is varied with respect to the metric. While this discussion is intended to illuminate the relation between
T^{\mu\nu}
and conserved quantities in the sense of Noether’s theorem, ultimately the quantity of interest for cosmology is
T^{\mu\nu}
, which appears in Einstein’s equation.

.

Frame-field formalism.

For theories whose matter action contains particles of half-integer spin, a covariant coupling to gravity cannot be accomplished within the metric formulation of general relativity. Instead, for these purposes it is customary to employ the frame-field formalism,
2
2

2

The frame field was introduced by
Cartan 1926
, who called them
repéres mobiles
(moving frames); in German they are referred to as
vierbeins
(four leg), and in the English literature they are usually called
tetrads
(Greek for “set of four”). Here we refer to them as frame fields.
which is an elegant and general formulation of general relativity that allows field theory with spinors to be extended to curved spacetime. Hereafter we follow the notation of
Freedman and Van Proeyen 2012
.

At any point on the spacetime manifold, one can find a basis that locally diagonalizes the metric, and with an appropriate rescaling this yields the metric for Minkowski spacetime
\eta_{ab}=\mathrm{s}_{1}\,\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,1,1)
. Requiring this diagonalization to be smooth leads to the relation

\begin{split}&{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}(x)=e_{\mu}^{a}(x)\,e_{\nu}^{b}(x)\,\eta_{ab}\ ,\ \end{split}

(147)

where
e_{\mu}^{a}(x)
for
a=1,2,3,4
is called the frame field.
3
3

3

Frame indices, also called local Lorentz indices, are denoted by the Latin letters
a
,
b
, etc., while coordinate indices are denoted by the Greek letters
\mu
,
\nu
, etc. Frame indices are raised and lowered by the Minkoski metric and its inverse
\eta_{ab}
and
\eta^{ab}
, while coordinate indices are raised and lowered by the local metric and its inverse
{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}
and
{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}
.
The decomposition of the metric
{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}(x)
into a pair of frame fields is not unique, and equivalent expressions are related by the local Lorentz transformations
e_{\mu}^{\prime a}(x)=\mathchoice{(\Lambda^{-1})^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{(\Lambda^{-1})^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{(\Lambda^{-1})^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{(\Lambda^{-1})^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}(x)e_{\mu}^{b}(x)
under which the frame index transforms and the coordinate index is inert. However, under coordinate transformations (diffeomorphisms) the frame field transforms as a set of four covariant vectors
e_{\mu}^{\prime a}(x^{\prime})=(\partial x^{\rho}/\partial x^{\prime\mu})e_{\rho}^{a}(x)
while the frame index is inert. Note that
\mathrm{det}(e)\equiv e=\sqrt{-g}
. There exists an inverse of the frame field, denoted by
e_{a}^{\mu}(x)
, such that
e_{\mu}^{a}e_{a}^{\nu}=\delta_{\mu}^{\nu}
and
e_{a}^{\mu}e_{\mu}^{b}=\delta_{a}^{b}
.

.

Spin connection.

Tensor fields of arbitrary rank can be expressed in the frame-field formalism. For example a contravariant vector field
V^{\mu}(x)
can be written as
V^{\mu}(x)=V^{a}(x)e_{a}^{\mu}(x)
where
V^{a}(x)=e_{\mu}^{a}(x)V^{\mu}(x)
are the frame components of the vector field, which transform as scalars under coordinate transformations and as a vector under local Lorentz transformations. The same holds for covariant vectors
V_{\mu}(x)=e_{\mu}^{a}(x)V_{a}(x)
where
V_{a}(x)=e_{a}^{\mu}(x)V_{\mu}(x)
. Derivatives of frame vector fields that transform covariantly under the local Lorentz transformations are given by

\begin{split}D_{\mu}V^{a}&=\partial_{\mu}V^{a}+(\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4})^{-1}\,\mathchoice{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}V^{b}\\
D_{\mu}V_{a}&=\partial_{\mu}V_{a}-(\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4})^{-1}\,\mathchoice{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}V_{b}\ ,\ \end{split}

(148)

where the appropriate affine connection, also known as the spin connection, is given by

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4}\,\Bigl[2\mathrm{e}^{\nu[a}\partial_{[\mu}\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.27318pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.27318pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.78163pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.70114pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b]}}}_{{{\nu]}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.78056pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.78056pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.46402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.47429pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.27318pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.27318pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.78163pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.70114pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b]}}}_{{{\nu]}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.78056pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.78056pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.46402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.47429pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.27318pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.27318pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.78163pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.70114pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b]}}}_{{{\nu]}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.78056pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.78056pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.46402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.47429pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.27318pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.27318pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.78163pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.70114pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b]}}}_{{{\nu]}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.78056pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.78056pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.46402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.47429pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}-\mathrm{e}^{\nu[a}\mathrm{e}^{b]\sigma}e_{\mu c}\partial_{\nu}e_{\sigma}^{c}\Bigr]\ .\

(149)

Here
X^{[a}Y^{b]}=(X^{a}Y^{b}-X^{b}Y^{a})/2
. The spin connection is required to be antisymmetric in its frame indices:
\mathchoice{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ab}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.693pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.35213pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ab}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.693pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.35213pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ab}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.693pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.35213pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ab}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.693pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.35213pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=-\mathchoice{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ba}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.693pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.35213pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ba}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.693pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.35213pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ba}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.693pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.35213pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ba}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.85431pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.693pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.35213pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
. The derivatives
\nabla_{\!\!\mu}V^{\nu}=e_{a}^{\nu}D_{\mu}V^{a}
and
\nabla_{\!\!\mu}V_{\nu}=e_{\nu}^{a}D_{\mu}V_{a}
are covariant under coordinate transformations, transforming as type
(1,1)
and
(0,2)
tensors, respectively. The Levi-Civita connection is related to the spin connection by
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\rho}=e_{a}^{\rho}\partial_{\mu}e_{\nu}^{a}+(\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4})^{-1}\,e_{a}^{\rho}\mathchoice{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}e_{\nu}^{b}
.

.

Spinor matrices.

Let
\gamma^{a}
for
a=0,1,2,3
denote the set of four complex
4\times 4
spinor matrices. They obey the Clifford algebra of anticommutation relations

\begin{split}\{\gamma^{a},\gamma^{b}\}&=\mathrm{s}_{6}\,2\eta^{ab}\mathbb{1}=(\mathrm{s}_{6}/\mathrm{s}_{1})\,2\,\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,1,1)\,\mathbb{1}\ ,\ \end{split}

(150)

where
\mathbb{1}
is the identity matrix on the spinor indices. For instance, in the Dirac representation

\displaystyle\gamma^{0}=\sqrt{\frac{-\mathrm{s}_{6}}{\mathrm{s}_{1}}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{1}&0\\
0&-\mathbb{1}\end{pmatrix},\quad\gamma^{i}=\sqrt{\frac{-\mathrm{s}_{6}}{\mathrm{s}_{1}}}\begin{pmatrix}0&\sigma^{i}\\
-\sigma^{i}&0\end{pmatrix}\ ,\

(151)

where
\sigma^{i}
for
i=1,2,3
are the Pauli matrices. One can also define

\displaystyle\gamma_{5}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{s}_{7}\,\mathrm{i}\gamma^{0}\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}\gamma^{3}

(152a)

\displaystyle\gamma^{ab}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{[a}\gamma^{b]}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\gamma^{a}\gamma^{b}-\gamma^{b}\gamma^{a})

(152b)

\displaystyle\gamma^{abc}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{[a}\gamma^{b}\gamma^{c]}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\gamma^{a}\gamma^{b}\gamma^{c}-\gamma^{c}\gamma^{b}\gamma^{a})\ ,\

(152c)

etc.; in addition,
\Sigma^{ab}=\gamma^{ab}/2
. Using the frame field, one defines a set of local gamma matrices

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}(x)=e_{a}^{\mu}(x)\,\gamma^{a}\ ,\

(153)

that satisfy the Clifford algebra locally

\displaystyle\{\gamma^{\mu}(x),\gamma^{\nu}(x)\}=\mathrm{s}_{6}\,2{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}(x)\mathbb{1}\ ,\

(154)

and from which one may construct
\gamma^{\mu\nu}(x)
and
\gamma^{\mu\nu\rho}(x)
in analogy with the previous expressions.

.

Spinor fields.

For spinor fields
\Psi(x)
or vector-spinor fields
\Psi_{\mu}(x)
one can define the covariant derivatives

\displaystyle D_{\mu}\Psi

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\Psi+\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4}\,\tfrac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu ab}\gamma^{ab}\Psi

(155a)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\!\!\mu}\Psi

\displaystyle=D_{\mu}\Psi

(155b)

\displaystyle D_{\mu}\Psi_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\Psi_{\nu}+\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4}\,\tfrac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu ab}\gamma^{ab}\Psi_{\nu}

(155c)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\!\!\mu}\Psi_{\nu}

\displaystyle=D_{\mu}\Psi_{\nu}-\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\rho}\Psi_{\rho}\ .\

(155d)

All four quantities transform as spinors under local Lorentz transformations, whereas only
\nabla_{\!\!\mu}\Psi_{\nu}
transforms as a type
(0,2)
tensor under coordinate transformations.

.

Conjugation.

The Hermitian conjugate of the spinor matrices, either
\gamma^{a}
or
\gamma^{\mu}(x)
, can be written as

\displaystyle(\gamma^{a})^{\dagger}=(\mathrm{s}_{1}\mathrm{s}_{\ast}/\mathrm{s}_{6})\,\gamma^{0}\gamma^{a}\gamma^{0}\ .\

(156)

The Clifford algebra implies that
(\gamma^{0})^{\dagger}=-\mathrm{s}_{\ast}\gamma^{0}
and
(\gamma^{i})^{\dagger}=\mathrm{s}_{\ast}\gamma^{i}
for
i=1,2,3
, such that
\gamma^{0}
is Hermitian for
\mathrm{s}_{\ast}=-1
and anti-Hermitian for
\mathrm{s}_{\ast}=+1
. Regardless of the sign choices,
(\gamma_{5})^{\dagger}=\gamma_{5}
. One can define the Dirac adjoint spinors

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}=(\mathrm{i}/\alpha)\,\Psi^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\Psi}_{\mu}=(\mathrm{i}/\alpha)\,\Psi_{\mu}^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}\ ,\

(157)

where
\gamma^{0}=\gamma^{a}|_{a=0}
is the constant spinor matrix and the phase
\alpha
takes values
\pm 1
or
\pm\mathrm{i}
. Covariant derivative of the Dirac adjoint spinors are defined by

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}\overleftarrow{D_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\bar{\Psi}-\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4}\,\tfrac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu ab}\bar{\Psi}\gamma^{ab}

(158a)

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}\overleftarrow{\nabla_{\!\!\mu}}

\displaystyle=\bar{\Psi}\overleftarrow{D_{\mu}}

(158b)

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}_{\nu}\overleftarrow{D_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\bar{\Psi}_{\nu}-\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4}\,\tfrac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu ab}\bar{\Psi}_{\nu}\gamma^{ab}

(158c)

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}_{\nu}\overleftarrow{\nabla_{\!\!\mu}}

\displaystyle=\bar{\Psi}_{\nu}\overleftarrow{D_{\mu}}-\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\rho}\Psi_{\rho}\ .\

(158d)

.

Bilinear products.

A final sign ambiguity arises in the complex conjugation of spinor products. If
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
are two anticommuting variables, such as components of a spinor field
\Psi
, then
\chi_{1}\chi_{2}=-\chi_{2}\chi_{1}
. The complex conjugate of their product may be evaluated as
(\chi_{1}\chi_{2})^{\ast}=-\beta\chi_{1}^{\ast}\chi_{2}^{\ast}=\beta\chi_{2}^{\ast}\chi_{1}^{\ast}
where the sign
\beta=\pm 1
controls whether complex conjugation also exchanges order. For example the bilinear product of two spinors complex conjugates to
(\bar{\psi}\chi)^{\ast}=\mathrm{s}_{8}\,\bar{\chi}\psi
, where
\mathrm{s}_{8}\equiv\beta\mathrm{s}_{\ast}\alpha/\alpha^{\ast}=\pm 1
. If
\mathrm{s}_{8}=+1
, then
\bar{\psi}\psi
is real,
i.e.
, complex conjugates to itself, but if
\mathrm{s}_{8}=-1
then
\bar{\psi}\psi
is imaginary and an additional factor of
\mathrm{i}=\sqrt{\mathrm{s}_{8}}
must be added when one constructs the Lagrangian mass term. A similar argument applies for kinetic terms.

.

Radiation-matter action.

The frame-field formalism may be used to study radiation-matter sectors containing fields of either integer or half-integer spin, but we employ it only in the study of spinor fields. The radiation-matter action for free massive fields of spin
s=0
,
\nicefrac{{1\mkern-2.2mu}}{{2}}
,
1
, and
\nicefrac{{3\mkern-2.2mu}}{{2}}
can be written as

\displaystyle S_{\text{\sc m}}

\displaystyle=\int\!\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\sqrt{-g}\,\sum_{s}\mathcal{L}^{(s)}

(159)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{s}_{1}\tfrac{1}{2}{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\!\!\mu}\Phi\nabla_{\!\!\nu}\Phi-\tfrac{1}{2}m^{2}\Phi^{2}-\mathrm{s}_{1}\mathrm{s}_{3}\tfrac{1}{2}\xi R\Phi^{2}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(\nicefrac{{1\mkern-2.2mu}}{{2}})}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mathrm{s}_{8}\mathrm{s}_{\ast}}\bigl(\tfrac{1}{4}\bar{\Psi}\gamma^{\mu}\nabla_{\!\!\mu}\Psi-\tfrac{1}{4}\bar{\Psi}\overleftarrow{\nabla_{\!\!\mu}}\gamma^{\mu}\Psi\bigr)-\sqrt{\mathrm{s}_{8}}\,\tfrac{1}{2}m\bar{\Psi}\Psi

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\textit{g}}^{\mu\rho}{\textit{g}}^{\nu\sigma}F_{\mu\nu}F_{\rho\sigma}-\mathrm{s}_{1}\tfrac{1}{2}m^{2}{\textit{g}}^{\mu\nu}A_{\mu}A_{\nu}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(\nicefrac{{3\mkern-2.2mu}}{{2}})}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mathrm{s}_{8}\mathrm{s}_{\ast}^{3}}\bigl(\tfrac{1}{4}\bar{\Psi}_{\mu}\,\gamma^{\mu\nu\rho}\nabla_{\!\!\nu}\Psi_{\rho}-\tfrac{1}{4}\bar{\Psi}_{\mu}\overleftarrow{\nabla_{\!\!\nu}}\gamma^{\mu\nu\rho}\Psi_{\rho}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad-\sqrt{\mathrm{s}_{8}\mathrm{s}_{\ast}^{2}}\,\tfrac{1}{2}m\bar{\Psi}_{\mu}\gamma^{\mu\nu}\Psi_{\nu}\ ,\

where
m^{2}
,
m
, and
\xi
are real parameters, and where

\displaystyle\nabla_{\!\!\mu}\Phi

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\Phi

(160a)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\!\!\mu}\Psi

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\Psi+\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4}\,\tfrac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu ab}\gamma^{ab}\Psi

(160b)

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{i}/\alpha)\,\Psi^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}

(160c)

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}\overleftarrow{\nabla_{\!\!\mu}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\bar{\Psi}-\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4}\,\tfrac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu ab}\bar{\Psi}\gamma^{ab}

(160d)

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\!\!\mu}A_{\nu}-\nabla_{\!\!\nu}A_{\mu}

(160e)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\!\!\mu}A_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\rho}A_{\rho}

(160f)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\!\!\mu}\Psi_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\Psi_{\nu}+\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4}\,\tfrac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu ab}\gamma^{ab}\Psi_{\nu}-\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\rho}\Psi_{\rho}

(160g)

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{i}/\alpha)\,\Psi_{\mu}^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}

(160h)

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}_{\mu}\overleftarrow{\nabla_{\!\!\nu}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\bar{\Psi}_{\nu}-\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4}\,\tfrac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu ab}\bar{\Psi}_{\nu}\gamma^{ab}-\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\rho}\bar{\Psi}_{\rho}\ .\

(160i)

Note that the
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\rho}
terms in
\nabla_{\!\!\mu}A_{\nu}
and
\nabla_{\!\!\mu}\Psi_{\nu}
do not contribute to the Lagrangian, since the symmetric Levi-Civita connection is contracted with an antisymmetric tensor. In the body of the review, we take
-\mathrm{i}\alpha=\beta=-\mathrm{s}_{\ast}=-\mathrm{s}_{1}=\mathrm{s}_{2}=\mathrm{s}_{3}=\mathrm{s}_{4}=\mathrm{s}_{6}=\mathrm{s}_{8}=1
.

Appendix B
FLRW spacetime

This appendix summarizes key relations that arise frequently in the FLRW spacetime. We provide expressions using both coordinate time
t
and conformal time
\eta
so that one can move between these two conventions. We consider only the spatially flat FLRW spacetime (
\mathrm{k}=0
).

.

Metric.

The differential spacetime interval for the homogeneous and isotropic FLRW spacetime
Friedmann 1922
;
Friedmann 1924
;
Lemaître 1931
;
Robertson 1935a
;
Robertson 1935b
;
Robertson 1936
;
Walker 1937
can be written as

\begin{split}(\mathrm{d}s)^{2}&=\mathrm{s}_{1}\bigl[-(\mathrm{d}t)^{2}+a^{2}(t)\,|\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}|^{2}\bigr]\quad\text{or}\\
(\mathrm{d}s)^{2}&=\mathrm{s}_{1}\bigl[-a^{2}(\eta)(\mathrm{d}\eta)^{2}+a^{2}(\eta)|\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}|^{2}\bigr]\ .\ \end{split}

(161)

We call
t
the time coordinate,
\eta
the conformal time coordinate,
{\bm{x}}
the comoving spatial coordinate, and
a
the scale factor. Note that
\mathrm{d}t=a(\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta
and
\mathrm{d}\eta=\mathrm{d}t/a(t)
, as well as
\partial_{t}=a^{-1}(\eta)\partial_{\eta}
and
\partial_{\eta}=a(t)\partial_{t}
. In other words, the nonzero components of the metric are

\begin{split}{\textit{g}}_{00}(t)=-\mathrm{s}_{1}\quad&\text{or}\quad{\textit{g}}_{00}(\eta)=-\mathrm{s}_{1}a^{2}(\eta)\\
{\textit{g}}_{ii}(t)=\mathrm{s}_{1}a^{2}(t)\quad&\text{or}\quad{\textit{g}}_{ii}(\eta)=\mathrm{s}_{1}a^{2}(\eta)\end{split}

(162)

for
i=1,2,3
(no summation). The metric determinant gives

\displaystyle\sqrt{-g}=a^{3}(t)\quad\text{or}\quad\sqrt{-g}=a^{4}(\eta)\ .\

(163)

When conformal time is used, the FLRW metric can be written compactly as
{\textit{g}}_{\mu\nu}(\eta)=a^{2}(\eta)\,\eta_{\mu\nu}
, where
\eta_{\mu\nu}
is the Minkowski metric. Hence, the (spatially flat) FLRW spacetime is conformally related to Minkowski spacetime. The components of the corresponding frame fields are

\begin{split}e_{\mu}^{0}(t)=\delta_{\mu}^{0}\quad&\text{or}\quad e_{\mu}^{0}(\eta)=a(\eta)\,\delta_{\mu}^{0}\\
e_{\mu}^{i}(t)=a(t)\,\delta_{\mu}^{i}\quad&\text{or}\quad e_{\mu}^{i}(\eta)=a(\eta)\,\delta_{\mu}^{i}\end{split}

(164)

for
\mu=0,1,2,3
and
i=1,2,3
.

.

Hubble parameter.

Evolution of the scale factor is measured with the Hubble parameter
H
, which is defined equivalently by both of the following relations:

\displaystyle H(t)=\frac{\dot{a}(t)}{a(t)}\quad\text{or}\quad H(\eta)=\frac{a^{\prime}(\eta)}{a^{2}(\eta)}\ .\

(165)

In Eq. (
165
), the dot denotes the derivative with respect to
t
and the prime denotes the derivative with respect to
\eta
. One can also define the comoving Hubble parameter
\mathcal{H}(\eta)=a^{\prime}(\eta)/a(\eta)
. The present-day value of the Hubble parameter, which is also known as the Hubble constant
Hubble 1929
, is denoted by
H_{0}
. It is customary to write
H_{0}=100h\ \mathrm{km}\,\,\mathrm{sec}^{-1}\,\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}
, where the dimensionless factor
h\approx 0.7\simeq 1/\sqrt{2}
.

.

Distances.

The distance between any two points in the expanding Universe changes in time in proportion to the scale factor
a
. If at some time the scale factor is
a_{1}
and the physical distance between two points is
l_{\mathrm{phys},1}
, then at some other time when the scale factor is
a
, the distance will be
l_{\mathrm{phys}}(a)=l_{\mathrm{phys},1}\,(a/a_{1})
. It is often useful to consider a comoving distance
l=l_{\mathrm{phys}}/a
; the comoving distance is constant in expansion. Similarly, the momentum of a particle redshifts in expansion as
{\bm{p}}_{\mathrm{phys}}(a)={\bm{p}}_{\mathrm{phys},1}\,(a_{1}/a)
, and it is useful to define a comoving momentum
{\bm{p}}=a\,{\bm{p}}_{\rm phys}
. Analogous relations arise when spatially varying fields are represented in Fourier space. A Fourier mode with a comoving wavevector
{\bm{k}}
and a comoving wave number
k=|{\bm{k}}|
corresponds to a comoving wavelength of
\lambda=2\pi/k
, and it corresponds to a physical wavelength of
\lambda_{\mathrm{phys}}=2\pi a/k
. We define the comoving Hubble length scale as

\displaystyle d_{H}(t)=\frac{2\pi}{a(t)H(t)}\quad\text{or}\quad d_{H}(\eta)=\frac{2\pi}{a(\eta)H(\eta)}\ .\

(166)

Modes with a comoving wavelength
\lambda
and a comoving wave number
k=2\pi/\lambda
are said to “cross” the Hubble scale (
i.e.
, “enter” or “leave” the horizon) when
k=aH
, corresponding to
\lambda=d_{H}
.

.

GR tensors.

In the FLRW spacetime, the only nonzero components of the Levi-Civita connection
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\rho}
are

\begin{split}\Gamma^{0}_{00}(t)=0\quad&\text{or}\quad\Gamma^{0}_{00}(\eta)=aH\\
\Gamma^{0}_{ii}(t)=a^{2}H\quad&\text{or}\quad\Gamma^{0}_{ii}(\eta)=aH\\
\Gamma^{i}_{0i}(t)=H\quad&\text{or}\quad\Gamma^{i}_{0i}(\eta)=aH\\
\Gamma^{i}_{i0}(t)=H\quad&\text{or}\quad\Gamma^{i}_{i0}(\eta)=aH\ ,\ \end{split}

(167)

for
i=1,2,3
(no summation). Similarly the nonzero components of the spin connection
\mathchoice{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
are

\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{s}_{1}}{\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4}}\,\mathchoice{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}{i}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}{i}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}{i}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}{i}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\texts
