ega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}{i}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(\eta)=aH\\
\frac{\mathrm{s}_{1}}{\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4}}\,\mathchoice{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(t)=-aH\quad&\text{or}\quad\frac{\mathrm{s}_{1}}{\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{s}_{4}}\,\mathchoice{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{0}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(\eta)=-aH\ .\ \end{split}

(168)

The nonzero components of the Ricci tensor are

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{3}\,R_{00}(t)=-3\frac{\ddot{a}}{a}\quad

\displaystyle\text{or}\quad\mathrm{s}_{3}\,R_{00}(\eta)=-3\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}+3\frac{a^{\prime 2}}{a^{2}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{3}\,R_{ii}(t)=a\ddot{a}+2\dot{a}^{2}\quad

\displaystyle\text{or}\quad\mathrm{s}_{3}\,R_{ii}(\eta)=\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}+\frac{a^{\prime 2}}{a^{2}}

(169)

for
i=1,2,3
(no summation). The Ricci scalar is

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{1}\mathrm{s}_{3}\,R(t)=6\frac{\ddot{a}}{a}+6\frac{\dot{a}^{2}}{a^{2}}\quad\text{or}\quad\mathrm{s}_{1}\mathrm{s}_{3}\,R(\eta)=6\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a^{3}}\ .\

(170)

The nonzero components of the Einstein tensor are

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{3}\,G_{00}(t)=3\frac{\dot{a}^{2}}{a^{2}}\quad

\displaystyle\text{or}\quad\mathrm{s}_{3}\,G_{00}(\eta)=3\frac{a^{\prime 2}}{a^{2}}

(171)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{3}\,G_{ii}(t)=-2a\ddot{a}-\dot{a}^{2}\quad

\displaystyle\text{or}\quad\mathrm{s}_{3}\,G_{ii}(\eta)=-2\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}+\frac{a^{\prime 2}}{a^{2}}

for
i=1,2,3
(no summation). For a perfect fluid with energy density
\rho
and pressure
P
, the stress-energy tensor in the fluid’s rest frame can be written as

\begin{split}T_{00}(t)=\rho&\quad\text{or}\quad T_{00}(\eta)=a^{2}\rho\\
T_{ii}(t)=a^{2}P&\quad\text{or}\quad T_{ii}(\eta)=a^{2}P\end{split}

(172)

for
i=1,2,3
(no summation). The diagonal entries of Einstein’s equation give

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{a}^{2}}{a^{2}}=\frac{1}{3M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}\rho\quad

\displaystyle\text{or}\quad\frac{a^{\prime 2}}{a^{4}}=\frac{1}{3M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}\rho

(173)

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{a}}{a}=-\frac{1}{6M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}(\rho+3P)\quad

\displaystyle\text{or}\quad\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a^{3}}=\frac{1}{6M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}(\rho-3P)\ ,\

which are Friedmann’s first and second equations. Differentiating the first equation in Eq. (
173
) gives the continuity equation

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}+3H(\rho+P)=0\quad\text{or}\quad\rho^{\prime}+3aH(\rho+P)=0\ ,\

(174)

which expresses energy nonconservation due to the cosmological expansion
H\neq 0
. Note that Friedmann’s equations allow the Ricci scalar to be written as

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{1}\mathrm{s}_{3}R=M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{-2}\bigl(\rho-3P\bigr)\ ,\

(175)

which holds using either coordinate or conformal time.

.

Constant equation of state.

Although the energy density
\rho
and pressure
P
may vary in time, it is often the case that their ratio, the equation of state
w=P/\rho
, is a constant. In that case the continuity equation is solved to find

\begin{split}\rho(a)&=\rho_{1}\bigl(a/a_{1}\bigr)^{-3(1+w)}\\
P(a)&=P_{1}\bigl(a/a_{1}\bigr)^{-3(1+w)}\\
H(a)&=H_{1}\bigl(a/a_{1}\bigr)^{-3(1+w)/2}\ ,\ \end{split}

(176)

where
P_{1}=w\rho_{1}
. Frequently encountered equations of state include:

•

Kination:

w=1
,
\rho\propto a^{-6}
, and
H\propto a^{-3}
.

•

Radiation:

w=\nicefrac{{1\mkern-2.2mu}}{{3}}
,
\rho\propto a^{-4}
, and
H\propto a^{-2}
.

•

Matter:

w=0
,
\rho\propto a^{-3}
, and
H\propto a^{-3/2}
.

•

Vacuum:

w=-1
,
\rho\propto a^{0}
, and
H\propto a^{0}
.

The cases labeled as
radiation
and
matter
refer more generally to relativistic and nonrelativistic particles.

Appendix C
Cosmological perturbations

This appendix contains an introduction to cosmological perturbations. The FLRW spacetime models an idealized homogeneous and isotropic universe, and describing our messy Universe requires a departure from FLRW. Provided that this departure is small, it can be treated perturbatively. Introductions to cosmological perturbation theory are given in various textbooks and review articles; we follow
Baumann 2022
.

.

Metric perturbations.

Allowing for perturbations on the FLRW metric, the spacetime interval may be written as

\begin{split}&(\mathrm{d}s)^{2}=\mathrm{s}_{1}\,a^{2}\bigl[-\bigl(1+2A\bigr)(\mathrm{d}\eta)^{2}+2B_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x^{i}\mathrm{d}\eta\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\bigl(\delta_{ij}+2E_{ij}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}x^{i}\mathrm{d}x^{j}\bigr]\\
&\quad\text{where}\quad B_{i}=\partial_{i}B+\hat{B}_{i}\\
&\quad\text{and}\quad E_{ij}=C\delta_{ij}+\partial_{\langle i}\partial_{j\rangle}E+\partial_{(i}\hat{E}_{j)}+\hat{E}_{ij}\ .\ \end{split}

(177)

Note that
\partial_{\langle i}\partial_{j\rangle}E=(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}-\tfrac{1}{3}\delta_{ij}\nabla^{2})E
and
\partial_{(i}\hat{E}_{j)}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{i}\hat{E}_{j}+\partial_{j}\hat{E}_{i})
. The functions
A(\eta,{\bm{x}})
,
B(\eta,{\bm{x}})
,
C(\eta,{\bm{x}})
, and
E(\eta,{\bm{x}})
are called the scalar metric perturbations;
\hat{B}_{i}(\eta,{\bm{x}})
and
\hat{E}_{i}(\eta,{\bm{x}})
are called the vector metric perturbations; and
\hat{E}_{ij}(\eta,{\bm{x}})
is called the tensor metric perturbation. The vector and tensor perturbations obey the transverse and traceless conditions:
\partial^{i}\hat{B}_{i}=\partial^{i}\hat{E}_{i}=\partial^{i}\hat{E}_{ij}=\mathchoice{\hat{E}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\hat{E}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\hat{E}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\hat{E}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}=0
.

.

Matter perturbations.

During and after inflation the Universe contained various particle species, including the inflaton, the standard-model particles, and the dark matter. We label the particle species with an index
s
. When interactions are neglected, the total stress-energy tensor can be expressed as a sum over the component stress-energy tensors associated with each species:
T_{\mu\nu}=\sum_{s}(T_{s})_{\mu\nu}
. The component stress-energy tensors may be decomposed as

\begin{split}\mathchoice{(T_{s})^{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}}{(T_{s})^{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}}{(T_{s})^{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}}{(T_{s})^{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}}&=-\mathrm{s}_{1}\,\bigl(\bar{\rho}_{s}+\delta\hskip-0.85355pt\rho_{s}\bigr)\\
\mathchoice{(T_{s})^{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{(T_{s})^{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{(T_{s})^{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{(T_{s})^{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}&=\mathrm{s}_{1}\,\bigl(\bar{\rho}_{s}+\bar{P}_{s}\bigr)v_{s,i}\\
\mathchoice{(T_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}}{(T_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}}{(T_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}}{(T_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{0}}}}&=-\mathrm{s}_{1}\,\bigl(\bar{\rho}_{s}+\bar{P}_{s}\bigr)v_{s,i}\\
\mathchoice{(T_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{(T_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{(T_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{(T_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}&=\mathrm{s}_{1}\,\bigl[\bigl(\bar{P}_{s}+\delta\hskip-0.85355ptP_{s}\bigr)\delta_{i}^{j}+\mathchoice{(\Pi_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{(\Pi_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{(\Pi_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{(\Pi_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}\bigr]\\
&\hskip-14.22636pt\text{where}\quad v_{s,i}=\partial_{i}v_{s}+\hat{v}_{s,i}\\
&\hskip-14.22636pt\text{and}\quad q_{s,i}=\partial_{i}q_{s}+\hat{q}_{s,i}\\
&\hskip-14.22636pt\text{and}\quad(\Pi_{s})_{ij}=\partial_{\langle i}\partial_{j\rangle}\Pi_{s}+\partial_{(i}\hat{\Pi}_{s,j)}+\hat{\Pi}_{s,ij}\ .\ \end{split}

(178)

The functions
\bar{\rho}_{s}(\eta)
and
\bar{P}_{s}(\eta)
are the background energy density and pressure,
\delta\hskip-0.85355pt\rho_{s}(\eta,{\bm{x}})
and
\delta\hskip-0.85355ptP_{s}(\eta,{\bm{x}})
are the energy density and pressure perturbations,
v_{s,i}(\eta,{\bm{x}})
is the velocity perturbation,
q_{s,i}(\eta,{\bm{x}})=(\bar{\rho}_{s}+\bar{P}_{s})v_{s,i}
is the momentum density perturbation, and
\mathchoice{[\Pi_{s}(\eta,{\bm{x}})]^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{[\Pi_{s}(\eta,{\bm{x}})]^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{[\Pi_{s}(\eta,{\bm{x}})]^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{[\Pi_{s}(\eta,{\bm{x}})]^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}
is the anisotropic stress perturbation. Note that the vector and tensor perturbations are transverse and traceless:
\partial^{i}\hat{v}_{s,i}=\partial^{i}\hat{q}_{s,i}=\partial^{i}(\Pi_{s})_{ij}=\mathchoice{(\Pi_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{(\Pi_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{(\Pi_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{(\Pi_{s})^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}=0
. One often frequently encounters the velocity divergence
\theta_{s}=\partial^{i}v_{s,i}
, the scaled anisotropic stress
(\sigma_{s})_{ij}=2(\Pi_{s})_{ij}/[3(\bar{\rho}_{s}+\bar{P}_{s})]
, and the energy density contrast
\delta_{s}=\delta\hskip-0.85355pt\rho_{s}/\bar{\rho}_{s}
.

.

Continuity equation.

The stress-energy tensor, which is expressed as
\nabla_{\!\!\mu}\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}=0
, is covariantly conserved. At the background level this leads to the energy continuity equation

\begin{split}\partial_{\eta}\bar{\rho}_{s}=-3\mathcal{H}(1+w_{s})\bar{\rho}_{s}\ ,\ \end{split}

(179)

where
w_{s}=\bar{P}_{s}/\bar{\rho}_{s}
is the equation of state.

.

Coordinate transformations.

Under a general coordinate transformation

\displaystyle x^{\mu}\mapstochar\rightarrow x^{\mu}+\xi^{\mu}(x)

(180)

where we can write
\xi^{0}=T
and
\xi^{i}=\partial^{i}L+\hat{L}^{i}
where
\partial^{i}\hat{L}_{i}=0
. The functions
T(\eta,{\bm{x}})
and
L(\eta,{\bm{x}})
parametrize the two scalar gauge freedoms, and the function
\hat{L}^{i}(\eta,{\bm{x}})
parametrizes the single vector gauge freedom. Invariance of the spacetime interval to linear order in
\xi^{\mu}
implies the following transformation rules for the metric perturbations:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow A-\partial_{\eta}T-\mathcal{H}T

(181a)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow B+T-\partial_{\eta}L

(181b)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow C-\mathcal{H}T-\tfrac{1}{3}\nabla^{2}L

(181c)

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow E-L

(181d)

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{i}

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow\hat{B}_{i}-\partial_{\eta}\hat{L}_{i}

(181e)

\displaystyle\hat{E}_{i}

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow\hat{E}_{i}-\hat{L}_{i}

(181f)

\displaystyle\hat{E}_{ij}

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow\hat{E}_{ij}\ .\

(181g)

Recall that
\mathcal{H}=aH
is the comoving Hubble parameter. Note that the tensor perturbations remain invariant. In addition, the matter perturbations transform as

\displaystyle\delta\hskip-0.85355pt\rho_{s}

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow\delta\hskip-0.85355pt\rho_{s}-\partial_{\eta}\bar{\rho}_{s}\,T

(182a)

\displaystyle\delta\hskip-0.85355ptP_{s}

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow\delta\hskip-0.85355ptP_{s}-\partial_{\eta}\bar{P}_{s}\,T

(182b)

\displaystyle q_{s,i}

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow q_{s,i}+\bigl(\bar{\rho}_{s}+\bar{P}_{s}\bigr)\,\partial_{\eta}L_{i}

(182c)

\displaystyle v_{s,i}

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow v_{s,i}+\partial_{\eta}L_{i}

(182d)

\displaystyle(\Pi_{s})_{ij}

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow(\Pi_{s})_{ij}\ .\

(182e)

Note that the anisotropic stress remains invariant.

.

Gauge fixing.

A judicious choice of the scalar transformation parameters
T(\eta,{\bm{x}})
and
L(\eta,{\bm{x}})
may be used to remove some of the scalar metric perturbations; this procedure is known as gauge fixing. Popular gauge choices have special names; for example, Newtonian gauge sets
B=E=0
, synchronous gauge sets
A=B=0
, spatially flat gauge sets
C=E=0
, uniform density gauge sets
\delta\hskip-0.85355pt\rho_{s}=0
, and comoving gauge sets
q_{s}=0
for some species
s
(typically the inflaton during inflation).

.

Gauge-invariant variables.

Whenever possible, it is best to work with quantities that are left unchanged under a coordinate transformation, at least to linear order in
\xi^{\mu}
; these are known as first-order gauge-invariant variables
Bardeen 1980
;
Bardeen et al. 1983
. Useful gauge-invariant variables include the comoving density contrast
\Delta(\eta,{\bm{x}})=\sum_{s}\Delta_{s}
, the curvature perturbation
\zeta(\eta,{\bm{x}})=\sum_{s}\zeta_{s}
, and the curvature perturbation
\mathcal{R}(\eta,{\bm{x}})=\sum_{s}\mathcal{R}_{s}
. Each of these quantities can be expressed as a sum over gauge-invariant components for each particle species,

\displaystyle\Delta_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta\hskip-0.85355pt\rho_{s}}{\bar{\rho}_{s}}+\frac{\partial_{\eta}\bar{\rho}_{s}}{\bar{\rho}_{s}}\,\bigl(v_{s}+B\bigr)

(183a)

\displaystyle\zeta_{s}

\displaystyle=-C+\tfrac{1}{3}\nabla^{2}E+\mathcal{H}\frac{\delta\hskip-0.85355pt\rho_{s}}{\partial_{\eta}\bar{\rho}_{s}}

(183b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{s}

\displaystyle=-C+\tfrac{1}{3}\nabla^{2}E-\mathcal{H}\,\bigl(v_{s}+B\bigr)\ .\

(183c)

Similarly, the relative curvature perturbation carried by two different particle species, denoted by
S_{sr}(\eta,{\bm{x}})
for species
s
and
r
, is a gauge-invariant variable known as the isocurvature perturbation,

\displaystyle S_{sr}=-3\bigl(\zeta_{s}-\zeta_{r}\bigr)=\frac{1}{1+w_{s}}\frac{\delta\hskip-0.85355pt\rho_{s}}{\bar{\rho}_{s}}-\frac{1}{1+w_{r}}\frac{\delta\hskip-0.85355pt\rho_{r}}{\bar{\rho}_{r}}\ ,\

(183d)

where we have used the energy continuity equation (
179
) in the second equality.

.

Adiabatic and isocurvature modes.

To solve for the evolution of the cosmological perturbations, it is necessary to set initial conditions for the various gauge-invariant variables. The initial condition is said to be in the adiabatic mode if
S_{sr}(\eta_{\mathrm{init}},{\bm{x}})=0
for all species
s
and
r
. This is a well-motivated assumption since it arises naturally in single-field inflation, where all of the various particle species arise from a common source, namely, the inflaton field. However, if any of the
S_{sr}
are allowed to be nonzero initially, then an isocurvature mode is said to be present.

.

Power spectra.

The two-point correlations of the curvature and isocurvature are encoded in the corresponding power spectra. We can write the power spectra as

\begin{split}\Delta_{XY}^{2}(\eta,k)=\frac{k^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}\int\!\mathrm{d}^{3}{\bm{r}}\,\bigl<0^{\text{\sc in}}\bigr|X(\eta,{\bm{x}})Y(\eta,{\bm{y}})\bigl|0^{\text{\sc in}}\bigr>\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{r}}}\end{split}

(184)

where
{\bm{y}}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{r}}
and where
X,Y
stand for either
\mathcal{R}
or
S
. For CMB studies it is customary to parametrize the initial curvature power spectrum as
\Delta_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}\equiv\Delta_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}}^{2}=A_{s}\,(k/k_{\text{\sc cmb}})^{n_{s}-1}
where the fiducial wave number
k_{\text{\sc cmb}}=0.05\,a_{0}\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}
corresponds to the typical scales probed by CMB observations.

Appendix D
de Sitter spacetime

This appendix contains expressions for solutions of the mode equations in de Sitter spacetime, a discussion of why CGPP does not take place in a de Sitter universe, and a discussion of applications to CGPP in an inflationary cosmology. This appendix is partly adapted from
Birrell and Davies 1984
.

.

de Sitter spacetime.

The de Sitter spacetime
de Sitter 1916a
;
de Sitter 1916b
;
de Sitter 1917
is defined as the set of points
(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})
that lie on the four-dimensional hyperboloid
z_{0}^{2}-z_{1}^{2}-z_{2}^{2}-z_{3}^{2}-z_{4}^{2}=-1/H^{2}
that is embedded in five-dimensional Minkowski spacetime with a length element

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}s)^{2}=\mathrm{s}_{1}\biggl[-(\mathrm{d}z_{0})^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})^{2}\biggr]\ .\

(185)

Points in de Sitter space can be written as

\begin{split}z_{0}&=(2\eta)^{-1}(\eta^{2}-x_{1}^{2}-x_{2}^{2}-x_{3}^{2}-1/H^{2})\\
z_{1}&=-(H\eta)^{-1}x_{1}\\
z_{2}&=-(H\eta)^{-1}x_{2}\\
z_{3}&=-(H\eta)^{-1}x_{3}\\
z_{4}&=(2\eta)^{-1}(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}-\eta^{2}-1/H^{2})\end{split}

(186)

using the four-dimensional coordinates
\eta,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in(-\infty,\infty)
, and the metric is

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}s)^{2}=\frac{\mathrm{s}_{1}}{H^{2}\eta^{2}}\Bigl[-(\mathrm{d}\eta)^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}(\mathrm{d}x_{i})^{2}\Bigr]\ .\

(187)

For
\eta\in(-\infty,0)
the distance between points of fixed comoving separation is growing like
-1/H\eta
as
\eta
increases, at
\eta=0
there is a coordinate singularity, and for
\eta\in(0,\infty)
the distance is shrinking. The de Sitter spacetime metric takes the same form as the spatially flat FLRW metric in Eq. (
161
) with
a(\eta)=-1/H\eta
. One can verify that the Hubble parameter and the Ricci scalar are constant:
H=a^{\prime}/a^{2}
and
\mathrm{s}_{1}\mathrm{s}_{3}R=12H^{2}
.

.

Mode functions.

In de Sitter spacetime with Hubble parameter
H
, the scalar field’s mode equation (
11
) becomes

\begin{split}&\chi_{k}^{\prime\prime}(\eta)+\omega_{k}^{2}(\eta)\,\chi_{k}(\eta)=0\qquad\text{where}\\
&\quad\omega_{k}^{2}(\eta)=k^{2}+\bigl(m^{2}/H^{2}-2+12\xi\bigr)/\eta^{2}\ .\ \end{split}

(188)

There is an infinite one-parameter family of models with
m^{2}/H^{2}-2+12\xi=0
for which the dispersion relation is independent of time
\omega_{k}^{2}=k^{2}
, the mode functions are the usual
\chi_{k}\propto\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}k\eta}
of Minkowski spacetime, and no particle production occurs. To study solutions for general parameters, one can first put this equation into a familiar form by defining
x=k\eta\in(-\infty,\infty)
,
y=\chi_{k}(\eta)/\sqrt{k\eta}
, and

\displaystyle\nu=\sqrt{\frac{9}{4}-\frac{m^{2}}{H^{2}}-12\xi}\ .\

(189)

With these substitutions the mode equation is recast in the form of Bessel’s equation,

\displaystyle x^{2}y^{\prime\prime}(x)+xy^{\prime}(x)+\bigl(x^{2}-\nu^{2}\bigr)\,y(x)=0\ .\

(190)

Its solutions are linear combinations of Bessel functions of the first and second kinds
J_{\nu}(x)
and
Y_{\nu}(x)
. In particular, we focus on Hankel functions of the first and second kinds
H_{\nu}^{(1,2)}(x)=J_{\nu}(x)\pm\mathrm{i}Y_{\nu}(x)
and write the solutions as

\begin{split}\chi_{k}(\eta)&=C_{1}\,\sqrt{-k\eta}\,H_{\nu}^{(1)}(-k\eta)\\
&\quad+C_{2}\ \sqrt{-k\eta}\ H_{\nu}^{(2)}(-k\eta)\ .\ \end{split}

(191)

Note that both the square-root function and the Hankel functions are double valued in the complex
k\eta
plane with a single branch point at
k\eta=0
, and we can orient their branch cuts to lie along the positive real axis,
k\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{>0}
. Similarly, as
m/H
and
\xi
are analytically continued into the complex plane,
\nu
is interpreted as the double-valued square-root function, and the Hankel functions may be evaluated with the complex index. Note that Hankel functions satisfy the relation
H_{-\nu}^{(1,2)}(z)=\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi\nu}H_{\nu}^{(1,2)}(z)
for
z,\nu\in\mathbb{C}
.

.

Bunch-Davies vacuum condition.

The
in
and
out
mode functions
\chi_{k}^{\text{\sc in}}(\eta)
and
\chi_{k}^{\text{\sc out}}(\eta)
correspond to the solutions that have the asymptotic forms in Eq. (
26
). The complex integration constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
can be chosen to yield the
in
mode function
\chi_{k}^{\text{\sc in}}(\eta)
. Hankel functions are asymptotic to
Abramowitz and Stegun 1964

\begin{split}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(z)&\sim\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{\pi z}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(z-\nu\pi/2-\pi/4)}\quad\text{as $|z|\rightarrow\infty$}\\
H_{\nu}^{(2)}(z)&\sim\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{\pi z}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(z-\nu\pi/2-\pi/4)}\quad\text{as $|z|\rightarrow\infty$}\end{split}

(192)

for
\mathrm{arg}(z)
throughout the first sheet, and for fixed
\nu
. Choosing
C_{1}=\sqrt{\pi/4k}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\nu\pi/2+\pi/4)}
and
C_{2}=0
gives

\begin{split}\chi_{k}^{\text{\sc in}}(\eta)&=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{4k}}\sqrt{-k\eta}\,H_{\nu}^{(1)}(-k\eta)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\nu\pi/2+\pi/4)}\ ,\ \end{split}

(193)

which is asymptotic to

\displaystyle\chi_{k}^{\text{\sc in}}(\eta)\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{2k}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}k\eta}

\displaystyle\quad\text{as}\quad k\eta\rightarrow-\infty

(194)

at early times for any value of
\nu
. For
k\eta\in(0,\infty)
we evaluate these functions above the cut on the first Riemann sheet. These mode functions solve the scalar field’s equation of motion in de Sitter spacetime along with the Bunch-Davies initial condition.

.

Special cases.

For models involving a massless scalar, or more generally
m\ll H
, the mode functions simplify. Here we regain expressions that are familiar from studies of scalar inflaton perturbations during inflation
Baumann 2022
. For the massless and conformally coupled scalar, setting
m=0
and
\xi=\nicefrac{{1\mkern-2.2mu}}{{6}}
yields

\begin{split}\chi_{k}^{\text{\sc in}}(\eta)&=\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{4k}}\sqrt{z}\,H_{1\mkern-2.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu2}^{(1)}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2k}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z}\ ,\ \end{split}

(195)

whereas for the massless and minimally coupled scalar, setting
m=0
and
\xi=0
yields

\begin{split}\chi_{k}^{\text{\sc in}}(\eta)=-\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{4k}}\sqrt{z}\,H_{3\mkern-2.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu2}^{(1)}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2k}}\left(1+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{z}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z}\end{split}

(196)

where
z\equiv-k\eta
.
As expected, the massless and conformally coupled scalar’s mode functions are equivalent to the positive-frequency mode functions in Minkowski spacetime; see the discussion in Sec.
V.1
. However, the minimally coupled scalar’s mode functions grow relatively by a factor of
1/k\eta
as modes leave the horizon, and
-1\ll k\eta<0
.

.

Connection to inflation.

During the epoch of inflation, the Hubble parameter varies slowly (
\epsilon=-H^{\prime}/aH^{2}\ll 1
), and the expanding branch of de Sitter spacetime is a good approximation for the spacetime. Consequently, the de Sitter mode functions (
193
) are a good approximation of the evolution of the spectator field. At early times they are compatible with the Bunch-Davies initial condition (
194
). At late times they progress to

\displaystyle\chi_{k}^{\text{\sc in}}(\eta_{e})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{4a_{e}H_{e}}}\,H_{\nu}^{(1)}(k/a_{e}H_{e})\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\nu\pi/2+\pi/4)}

(197a)

\displaystyle|\chi_{k}^{\text{\sc in}}(\eta_{e})|

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2k}}\quad\text{for $m=0$, $\xi=\nicefrac{{1\mkern-2.2mu}}{{6}}$}

(197b)

\displaystyle|\chi_{k}^{\text{\sc in}}(\eta_{e})|

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2k}}\sqrt{1+\dfrac{a_{e}^{2}H_{e}^{2}}{k^{2}}}\quad\text{for $m=0$, $\xi=0$}\ ,\

(197c)

where we have defined the “end of inflation” as
\eta_{e}=-1/a_{e}H_{e}
. Although the Hubble parameter begins to vary significantly at the end of inflation, thus invalidating the de Sitter solution, these expressions typically provide reliable approximations that can be used as initial conditions for the mode equations during reheating after inflation.

Appendix E
JWKB Method

This appendix contains a review of the Jeffreys-Wentzel-Kramers-Brillouin method
Jeffreys 1925
;
Wentzel 1926
;
Kramers 1926
;
Brillouin 1926
. The JWKB method can be applied to derive approximate solutions to equations of the form

\displaystyle y^{\prime\prime}(x)+Q(x)\,y(x)=0

(198)

where
Q(x)
is a differentiable function. See
Bender and Orszag 1999
for an introduction to asymptotic methods and perturbation theory, and see
Parker and Toms 2009
for applications of JWKB to QFT in curved spacetime (such as adiabatic subtraction and trace anomaly).

.

Motivation.

Equations of this form arise in the description of both classical and quantum physical systems. In classical mechanics the displacement of a one-dimensional harmonic oscillator with a time-dependent natural frequency is governed by
d^{2}x/dt^{2}+\omega^{2}(t)x(t)=0
. In quantum mechanics the wave function of a nonrelativistic mass moving in a one-dimensional static potential is governed by
d^{2}\psi/dx^{2}+(2m/\hbar^{2})[E-V(x)]\,\psi(x)=0
. In quantum field theory the Fourier mode amplitudes of a free scalar field in an FLRW spacetime are governed by
\chi_{k}^{\prime\prime}(\eta)+\omega_{k}^{2}(\eta)\,\chi_{k}(\eta)=0
, which we encountered in Eq. (
11
). Similar second-order linear equations appear in the study of free fields with spin; see Sec.
V
.

.

Strategy.

The JWKB method draws upon perturbation theory and asymptotic series to tackle equations of the form of Eq. (
198
) (as well as more general equations). To appreciate the strategy note that if
Q(x)=q^{2}
is a constant, one can immediately identify the four-dimensional space of solutions
y(x)=C_{1}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}qx}+C_{2}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}qx}
for arbitrary complex coefficients
C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
. This observation motivates one to seek a solution for general
Q(x)
using perturbation theory, with the constant case taken as the expansion point. The JWKB method entails the construction of a power series. With the exception of special cases, such as the constant
Q(x)=q^{2}
, the resultant power series is divergent. However the first few terms in this series typically provide a good approximation to the desired solution. In this appendix we explain the conceptual underpinnings of the JWKB method and remark upon its application to QFT in curved spacetime.

.

Adiabatic parameter.

Since Eq. (
198
) is easily solved for the constant
Q(x)
, this suggests that approximate solutions should be available for slowly varying
Q(x)
with small time derivatives. To seek out such solutions, introduce an expansion parameter
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
at each appearance of a derivative:
\partial_{x}\rightarrow\varepsilon\,\partial_{x}
. In principle
Q(x)
may be constructed from derivatives as well, so we should write
Q(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{n}Q_{n}(x)
. With these substitutions one obtains an infinite one-parameter family of equations,
4
4

4

It may appear that introducing
\varepsilon
has made the problem more difficult since, rather than needing to solve the single equation (
198
), it is now necessary to solve an infinite family of equations. However, if we assume that the solutions of Eq. (
199
) can be expressed as a Taylor series in powers of an arbitrary
\varepsilon
, then we can solve order by order in powers of
\varepsilon
. In this way perturbation theory substitutes one hard problem for an infinite number of easy problems
Bender and Orszag 1999
.

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\,y^{\prime\prime}(x;\varepsilon)+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{n}Q_{n}(x)\,y(x;\varepsilon)=0\ .\

(199)

Once these equations are solved, the desired solution is
y(x;1)
. Since
\varepsilon
tracks time derivatives, it is called the adiabatic parameter (or
T=\varepsilon^{-1}
, the slowness parameter), and
O(\varepsilon^{n})
terms are of
n
th adiabatic order.

.

JWKB ansatz.

Since the equation with the constant
Q(x)
is solved using an exponential function, this observation motivates one to search for solutions that can be written as

\displaystyle\tilde{y}(x;\varepsilon)=\mathrm{exp}\biggl(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{n}S_{n}(x)\biggr)\ .\

(200)

It may not be the case that solutions of Eq. (
199
) can be written in this form. Nevertheless, it is valuable to search for such solutions since, even if they do not solve the equation, they may provide a good approximation to the desired solution. Putting this ansatz into the equation for
y(x;\varepsilon)
and solving order by order in powers of
\varepsilon
yields

\begin{split}[S_{0}^{\prime}(x)]^{2}&=-Q_{0}(x)\\
2S_{0}^{\prime}(x)S_{1}^{\prime}(x)&=-Q_{1}(x)-S_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x)\\
2S_{0}^{\prime}(x)S_{2}^{\prime}(x)&=-Q_{2}(x)-S_{1}^{\prime\prime}(x)-[S_{1}^{\prime}(x)]^{2}\ ,\ \end{split}

(201)

etc. Note that there are two solution branches corresponding to
S_{0}(x)=\mp\int^{x}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\sqrt{-Q_{0}(x^{\prime})}
.

.

Discussion.

If the series
\tilde{y}(x;\varepsilon)
is convergent at
\varepsilon=1
, then
y(x)=\tilde{y}(x;1)
is the desired solution to the original equation. However, for the situations of interest, the series typically diverges for any nonzero
\varepsilon
. In that case the formal series should be understood as an asymptotic expansion of the solution at small adiabatic parameter,

\displaystyle y(x;\varepsilon)\sim\tilde{y}(x;\varepsilon)\quad\text{as}\quad\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\ .\

(202)

This outcome may seem like a failure since the goal was to solve the equation with
\varepsilon=1
, but Eq. (
202
) gives the asymptotic form of the solution as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Nevertheless, it is often the case that an asymptotic series, when truncated to a low order, provides a good approximation. For instance, truncating the series at
O(\varepsilon^{N})
and taking
\varepsilon=1
results in the function

\displaystyle y^{(N)}(x)=\mathrm{exp}\biggl(\,\sum_{n=0}^{N}S_{n}(x)\biggr)\ .\

(203)

To be concrete suppose that
Q_{0}(x)=[q(x)]^{2}
and
Q_{n}(x)=0
for
n\geq 1
. For
N=1
we then have

\displaystyle y^{(1)}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2q(x)}}\,\mathrm{exp}\biggl(-\mathrm{i}\int^{x}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\,q(x)\biggr)\ ,\

(204)

which is the leading-order JWKB approximation. Although
y^{(1)}(x)
is not a solution of Eq. (
198
) (except for special cases like the constant
q(x)
), it typically provides a good approximation. Adding a few more terms in the asymptotic series will typically improve the approximation, but eventually adding more terms will expose the divergence of the series and cause the approximation to degrade. The onset of the divergence is controlled by the magnitude of the derivatives of
q(x)
: since
y^{(1)}(x)
is an exact solution for the constant
q(x)
, one expects
y^{(1)}(x)
to be a good approximation when
q(x)
is slowly varying,
i.e.
, adiabatic. It is customary to define a dimensionless measure of the adiabaticity of the system as

\displaystyle A(x)=|q^{\prime}(x)/q(x)^{2}|\ ,\

(205)

which corresponds to the fraction by which
q(x)
changes during each oscillation cycle. Smaller
A(x)
corresponds to a more adiabatic system, and
y^{(1)}(x)
provides a better approximation of the desired solution.

.

Applications to QFT in curved spacetime.

In the remainder of this appendix, we focus on applications of the JWKB method to studies of QFT in curved spacetime. For concreteness we use the scalar field in FLRW spacetime as an example. The scalar field’s Fourier mode amplitudes
\chi_{k}(\eta)
obey the second-order, linear differential equation (
11
). When the adiabatic parameter
\varepsilon
is introduced, the equation of motion becomes

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\,\chi^{\prime\prime}(\eta;\varepsilon)+\omega^{2}(\eta;\varepsilon)\,\chi(\eta;\varepsilon)=0\quad\text{with}\quad

(206)

\displaystyle\omega^{2}(\eta;\varepsilon)=\bigl[k^{2}+a^{2}(\eta)m^{2}\bigr]+\bigl[(\tfrac{1}{6}-\xi)a^{2}(\eta)R(\eta)\bigr]\,\varepsilon^{2}\ ,\

where we have dropped the subscript
k
to simplify the notation. Since the Ricci scalar
R(\eta)
involves two time derivatives, this term develops a factor of
\varepsilon^{2}
. For studies of QFT in curved spacetime, it is customary to write the JWKB ansatz as
Birrell and Davies 1984

\begin{split}&\tilde{\chi}(\eta;\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2W(\eta;\varepsilon)}}\,\exp\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\varepsilon}\int^{\eta}\!\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}\,W(\eta^{\prime};\varepsilon)\right)\end{split}

(207)

where the power series
W(\eta;\varepsilon)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{n}W_{n}(\eta)
satisfies

\displaystyle\bigl[W(\eta,\varepsilon)\bigr]^{2}=\omega^{2}(\eta;\varepsilon)-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\biggl[\frac{W^{\prime\prime}(\eta;\varepsilon)}{W(\eta;\varepsilon)}-\frac{3}{2}\biggl(\frac{W^{\prime}(\eta;\varepsilon)}{W(\eta;\varepsilon)}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]\ .\

(208)

Equating order by order in powers of
\varepsilon
yields

\displaystyle\bigl[W_{0}(\eta)\bigr]^{2}

\displaystyle=k^{2}+a^{2}(\eta)m^{2}\equiv[\omega_{0}(\eta)]^{2}

\displaystyle 2W_{0}(\eta)W_{2}(\eta)

\displaystyle=(\tfrac{1}{6}-\xi)a^{2}(\eta)R(\eta)

\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{3}{4}\left(\frac{W_{0}^{\prime}(\eta)}{W_{0}(\eta)}\right)^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\frac{W_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\eta)}{W_{0}(\eta)}\ ,\

(209)

and
W_{n}(\eta)=0
for odd
n
. Once again there are two solution branches, with
W_{0}(\eta)=\pm\omega_{0}(\eta)
, which correspond to positive- and negative-frequency modes, respectively. The
N
th-order JWKB approximation is obtained by truncating the series at
O(\varepsilon^{N})
and setting
\varepsilon=1
,

\displaystyle\chi^{(N)}(\eta)=\frac{\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\int^{\eta}\!\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}\,\sum_{n=0}^{N}W_{n}(\eta^{\prime})\right)}{\sqrt{2\sum_{n=0}^{N}W_{n}(\eta)}}\ .\

(210)

For instance, the leading-order JWKB approximation is

\displaystyle\chi^{(0)}(\eta)=\frac{\exp\Bigl(-\mathrm{i}\int^{\eta}\!\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}\,\sqrt{k^{2}+a^{2}(\eta^{\prime})m^{2}}\Bigr)}{\bigl[2\sqrt{k^{2}+a(\eta)^{2}m^{2}}\bigr]^{1/2}}\ .\

(211)

.

Adiabatic vacuum.

One application of the JWKB method for QFT in curved spacetime is the definition of the adiabatic vacuum
Birrell and Davies 1984
;
Mukhanov and Winitzki 2007
. Although the JWKB approximation is not a solution of the original equation, it can be used as an initial condition at conformal time
\eta_{0}
to define an exact solution,

\displaystyle\chi(\eta_{0})=\chi^{(N)}(\eta_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\chi^{\prime}(\eta_{0})=\frac{\mathrm{d}\chi^{(N)}}{\mathrm{d}\eta}\Bigr|_{\eta=\eta_{0}}\ .\

(212)

The solution consistent with these initial conditions is known as the
N
th-order adiabatic mode function at time
\eta_{0}
. The vacuum state annihilated by the corresponding ladder operators is known as the
N
th-order adiabatic vacuum,
\bigl|0_{\mathrm{ad},\eta_{0}}\bigr>
. If the system is strongly adiabatic at early times,
i.e.
,
\omega^{\prime}(\eta)/\omega^{2}(\eta)\rightarrow 0
as
\eta\rightarrow-\infty
, then all the vacuum prescriptions agree asymptotically, thereby providing a naturally unique vacuum state.

.

Adiabatic regularization.

The construction of adiabatic orders through the JWKB method also proves useful for handling UV divergences through the technique of adiabatic regularization
Parker and Toms 2009
. Calculating observables that are nonlinear in the fields, such as expectation values of the stress-energy tensor, generally leads to UV divergences. Adiabatic regularization entails expressing the observable as a power series in the adiabatic parameter
\varepsilon
and removing all of the terms at a given order if any of those terms contain a UV divergence. For a schematic example suppose that we calculate an observable and find that it can be written as
O=(a_{0})\varepsilon^{0}+(a_{2}+b_{2}+c_{2})\varepsilon^{2}+(a_{4}+b_{4})\varepsilon^{4}+\cdots
, where the coefficients represent momentum integrals. Suppose that
a_{0}
and
b_{2}
are integrals with UV divergences while the other terms are finite. Adiabatic regularization then requires the removal of all terms at
\varepsilon^{0}
and
\varepsilon^{2}
, leaving only
O_{\mathrm{reg}}=(a_{4}+b_{4})\varepsilon^{4}+\cdots
. Adiabatic regularization proves useful in several derivations, including the conformal anomaly in the trace of the stress-energy tensor.

.

Extensions.

Whereas this discussion has focused on the JWKB method for equations of the form of Eq. (
198
), which arise in bosonic field theories
Ferreiro and Torrenti 2023
;
Ferreiro et al. 2023
;
Marañón González and Navarro-Salas 2023
, a similar method is available for the linear, coupled equations that arise in theories of fermions. See
Durrer et al. 2009
;
Dumlu and Dunne 2010
;
Landete et al. 2013
;
Landete et al. 2014
;
del Rio et al. 2014
;
Dabrowski and Dunne 2014
;
Dabrowski and Dunne 2016
;
Enomoto and Matsuda 2021
;
Sou et al. 2021
;
Enomoto and Matsuda 2022
;
Hashiba et al. 2022
;
Corbà and Sorbo 2023
. See
Sola Peracaula 2022
and references therein for a discussion of the implications of the cosmological constant problem.
